French PM blames ‘ultra-left’ for exploiting protests as he seeks partial curfews – Europe live
Sébastien Lecornu says new measures will be put in place from tonight
Macron criticises ‘unbridled violence’ after 2,400 arrests across France
Mon 19 Oct 2026 08.15 EDT First published on Mon 19 Oct 2026 03.44 EDT
Swedish centrist leader Thand Ringqvist tasked with leading exploratory talks on new government
EU condemns &#x27;looting&#x27; in France, says right to peaceful protest &#x27;must be protected&#x27;
Lecornu blames &#x27;ultra-left&#x27; and criminals for exploiting protests
French PM briefing media after crisis meeting on protests
Italy&#x27;s Meloni to hold crisis talks after clashes with police in Milan, Turin, Bologna
Protesters block Louvre entrances in Paris as they back student campaign
Marseille bans public gatherings, demonstrations today
Zelenskyy confirms no intelligence on overnight attacks, says US &#x27;in denial&#x27; about Russia&#x27;s position
Police clashes with protesters as protests spread to Italy, as fuel, fresh food shortages also hit south
Morning opening: Macron declines offer of interior minister&#x27;s resignation after weekend riots
A fire is seen with the Eiffel Tower in the background during clashes on Saturday Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images
A fire is seen with the Eiffel Tower in the background during clashes on Saturday Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images
French PM Lecornu says he has asked prefects to implement “a number of partial curfews” in certain “localised” urban areas to stop further disorder, which he attributes to “the extreme-left” and some criminal elements.
France’s prime minister Sébastien Lecornu looks on before an emergency meeting in Paris. Photograph: Julien de Rosa/AFP/Getty Images We will no doubt hear more on this from local authorities, as in the example of Marseille ( 10:32 ).
He also says the government is determined to respond to the events as needed, and if declaring a state of emergency is deemed necessary, they would do so – but that’s a major decision to make and currently not being considered.
He says be believes “there is an agenda of chaos being pushed by the far-left,” with the intention of disrupting next year’s presidential election, and that’s why he urges the NR leaders to attend his briefing later today, despite their plans no to do so ( 12:20 ).
Lecornu also says that there will be a “strong” response from the country’s judiciary, cracking down on the violence and crime on the margins of the protests. Investigators are also looking through social media footage, where rioters often did not wear masks.
New measures to stop riots from reoccurring will be put in place starting tonight, but tomorrow’s football game between PSG and Barcelona is currently scheduled to still go ahead.
He also says that furlough measures for businesses affected by riots will kick in on Tuesday to ensure all jobs are saved despite any disruption to business operations in the build up to the busy Christmas period.
He says there will also be further targeted measures, but with little detail at this stage.
Separately, the government will also push to expedite various assessments required to pay out any insurance claims.
Lecornu also says that the right to legitimate protest needs to be safeguarded and considered separately from the riots and clashes with police forces.
Swedish centrist leader Thand Ringqvist tasked with leading exploratory talks on new government
EU condemns &#x27;looting&#x27; in France, says right to peaceful protest &#x27;must be protected&#x27;
Lecornu blames &#x27;ultra-left&#x27; and criminals for exploiting protests
French PM briefing media after crisis meeting on protests
Italy&#x27;s Meloni to hold crisis talks after clashes with police in Milan, Turin, Bologna
Protesters block Louvre entrances in Paris as they back student campaign
Marseille bans public gatherings, demonstrations today
Zelenskyy confirms no intelligence on overnight attacks, says US &#x27;in denial&#x27; about Russia&#x27;s position
Police clashes with protesters as protests spread to Italy, as fuel, fresh food shortages also hit south
Morning opening: Macron declines offer of interior minister&#x27;s resignation after weekend riots
The British prime minister has added his voice to the chorus of politicians condemning the violence in France .
A spokesperson for Andy Burnham in Downing Street said “Clearly, the violence we’re seeing on the streets of France is unacceptable. While the power to protest is at the heart of democracy, this can never excuse violence.”
British nationals in France were told by the UK government to “follow local advice from the authorities” if they were concerned about the situation.
German police have said that two men turned themselves in after a security fence was damaged at the airport in the city of Leipzig. The incident had sparked fears as it took place at the same airport where an attack with an explosives-laden drone happened in August, an incident German authorities attributed to Russia .
Authorities had been investigating how a gaping hole was cut into a fence around the airport. The two men say they lost control of the car and crashed through the fence, then left the scene of the accident and arranged for the car to be towed away. AP reports that German police say the car has been seized and the investigation is continuing.
Investigators inspect the site of the damaged fence. Photograph: Heiko Rebsch/DPA/AP As promised, we have an update from Sweden , with the Centre party’s Elisabeth Thand Ringqvist given a chance to lead talks about the new government in the country.
Centre Party leader Elisabeth Thand Ringqvist, first right, during a televised debate before last month’s general election. Photograph: Jonathan Nackstrand/AFP/Getty Images Thand Ringqvist welcomed the opportunity to lead the discussions and said she would look to progress the talks, but made it clear she had no intention of becoming the next prime minister.
She will try to break a deadlock among the four opposition parties that emerged with a theoretical majority in the new parliament after last month’s elections, but continue to disagree on the composition of the future government.
The Centre party has ruled out being in government with the Left, while the Left has demanded a place in government in return for its cooperation. Without an agreement, the opposition bloc would not have a majority in the new parliament.
Thand Ringqvist will have to report back on the progress of her talks by the end of the week.
The move by the parliament’s speaker Andreas Norlén comes days after the Social Democrats leader and the September election winner Magdalena Andersson failed in her efforts to unite the opposition in the first attempt.
Outgoing prime minister Ulf Kristersson told Swedish broadcaster SVT it was the “logical” decision to give the theoretical opposition bloc another go at forming the next administration.
The European Commission has condemned “unacceptable” acts of “arson, looting and attacks on people or public buildings” in France over the weekend, but stressed the right to peaceful protests was “a fundamental right … and must be protected.”
Police clash with protesters during a demonstration amid a nationwide mobilisation over rising living costs, fuel prices and education funding in Paris, France. Photograph: Hesham Elsherif/Anadolu/Getty Images EU internal affairs spokesperson Markus Lammert said:
“The right to peaceful protest is a fundamental right in all democratic countries and must be protected. Those who demonstrate peacefully must not be conflated with those who commit violence. Arson, looting and attacks on people or public buildings are unacceptable.
We support the efforts of the French authorities to protect people, property and public institutions in full respect of fundamental rights.
It is national authorities that are competent for law enforcement matters, and investigations are currently ongoing at national level.”
French PM Lecornu says he has asked prefects to implement “a number of partial curfews” in certain “localised” urban areas to stop further disorder, which he attributes to “the extreme-left” and some criminal elements.
France’s prime minister Sébastien Lecornu looks on before an emergency meeting in Paris. Photograph: Julien de Rosa/AFP/Getty Images We will no doubt hear more on this from local authorities, as in the example of Marseille ( 10:32 ).
He also says the government is determined to respond to the events as needed, and if declaring a state of emergency is deemed necessary, they would do so – but that’s a major decision to make and currently not being considered.
He says be believes “there is an agenda of chaos being pushed by the far-left,” with the intention of disrupting next year’s presidential election, and that’s why he urges the NR leaders to attend his briefing later today, despite their plans no to do so ( 12:20 ).
Lecornu also says that there will be a “strong” response from the country’s judiciary, cracking down on the violence and crime on the margins of the protests. Investigators are also looking through social media footage, where rioters often did not wear masks.
New measures to stop riots from reoccurring will be put in place starting tonight, but tomorrow’s football game between PSG and Barcelona is currently scheduled to still go ahead.
He also says that furlough measures for businesses affected by riots will kick in on Tuesday to ensure all jobs are saved despite any disruption to business operations in the build up to the busy Christmas period.
He says there will also be further targeted measures, but with little detail at this stage.
Separately, the government will also push to expedite various assessments required to pay out any insurance claims.
Lecornu also says that the right to legitimate protest needs to be safeguarded and considered separately from the riots and clashes with police forces.
Sébastien Lecornu gives briefing after crisis meeting on violent protests – watch live
We are expecting to hear from France’s prime minister Sébastien Lecornu later today, but he is currently chairing an emergency meeting on the events of the weekend.
France's interior Minister Laurent Nuñez and prime minister Sebastien Lecornu attend an interministerial crisis meeting in Paris, France. Photograph: Julien de Rosa/EPA Party leaders will also meet with the PM to discuss the political fallout from the protests.
Curiously, the far-right National Rally party will not be represented neither by its presidential candidate, Marine Le Pen, nor its prime ministerial candidate, Jordan Bardella, but a more junior figure, media reported.
In the meantime, Paris prosecutor said at least 145 people were still in custody following “various acts of vandalism, looting and attacks on law enforcement,” as reported by Le Figaro.
A person wears a mask near burning bicycles during civil unrest, the day after a national day of demonstrations against the cost of living and rising fuel prices, in Paris, France. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters in Rome
Italian prime minister Giorgia Meloni will hold a crisis meeting with top security officials on Monday after two days of ransacking and looting in Milan allegedly perpetrated by gangs of hooded youths.
The unrest began on Saturday on the fringes of a protest against the rising cost of living. There were also clashes between police and protesters in Turin and Bologna.
Clashes in Bologna last weekend. Photograph: Alessandro Ruggeri/LaPresse/Shutterstock In Milan, more than 200 were detained by police, allegedly responsible for ransacking shops and smashing windows, including in the area surrounding the city’s Duomo. According to reports in the Italian press on Monday, 80% were minors, with many responding to appointments made on social media and sharing selfies as they bragged about the damage caused.
They were not believed to have been affiliated with any political party or organised groups or movements.
Meloni said on Saturday that the perpetrators were “thugs who exploit demonstrations to spread chaos, attack those who guarantee security, and hold entire cities to hostage”.
“To demonstrate is a right. To lay our cities to waste is not,” she wrote on X.
Police patrol streets after clashes with youth protesters near Piazza del Duomo in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Alessandro Bremec/NurPhoto/Shutterstock During her more than four years in power, Meloni has enacted tough security measures, including against peaceful protesters.
In July, her cabinet approved so-called “anti-maranza” legislation in response to a rise in child gangs and youth delinquency in city centres. “Maranza” is a slang term used to describe gangs of delinquent youths, usually coming from suburban areas.
Opposition politicians often accuse the government of using security as a propaganda tool, arguing that Italy’s leaders do little to address core issues such as education, housing and living conditions in marginalised areas.
There are no signs of French protests stopping anytime soon: the yellow vest movement, de gilets jaunes , has just announced a “second act” of protests for upcoming Saturday, 24 October – and then again a week after that, too.
The idea is to “keep the momentum going” until another major demonstration planned for 5 November, a group member told AFP.
A car burns near a graffiti reading "introduction to civil war" as clashes erupted during a demonstration against the "high cost of living", in Paris last weekend. Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images Protesters blocked entrances to the Louvre museum in Paris in support of a nationwide high school student protest movement and to denounce “police repression”, the museum’s management and unions told AFP.
The museum could not open as scheduled because of a protest led by students from the Ecole du Louvre, management said. A leaflet displayed outside the museum read: “We are proud to announce that we are blocking the world’s largest museum.”
Protesters and students from art and culture schools block the entrance to the Louvre in Paris, France. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters Back to France , the authorities in Marseille have just issued a ban of any public gatherings and demonstrations “throughout the entire commune” from midday to 8pm.
Violent clashes and damage occurred before the start of a protest on the Canebiere and at the Old Port against the high cost of living in Marseille, France. Photograph: Alain ROBERT/SIPA/Shutterstock Meanwhile, Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy has responded to the latest wave of Russian attacks on Ukraine, confirming Ukraine had no prior warning from the US and seemingly accusing Washington of being in the state of denial about the reality of the war.
A woman stands next to a damaged beauty store near a metro station, which was hit by an overnight Russian missile strike, amid Russia's attack on Ukraine, in Kyiv, Ukraine. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters “Another Russian ballistic missile attack came in several waves overnight, and once again, there was no early warning from our partners, ” he said.
“They again targeted energy facilities and infrastructure, but a medical clinic, a school, a metro station, and ordinary residential buildings were also damaged.”
Showing growing frustration with the attacks and continued criticism from US president Donald Trump , he said:
“ Ukraine never wanted this war, and we have welcomed literally EVERY attempt by our partners to bring this war to a dignified end. We have responded positively to EVERY ceasefire proposal. Russia’s response, time and again, has been ballistic missiles, “shaheds,” and killings.
Putin not only doesn’t want to end this war – he doesn’t even want to take any steps toward de-escalation. EVERY ONE of our partners has experienced this firsthand when they heard Russia’s refusals.
And it is crucial to call things what they are. To call the aggressor an aggressor and help Ukraine defend lives. When deliveries of missiles for Ukraine’s air defence are blocked while Russia is granted relief or delays in sanctions, this is, unfortunately, a denial of reality on the part of our partners.
The reality is that the war continues, and it continues solely because of Russian strikes and Russia’s desire to drag this war out.”
Italian police also clashed with protesters who threw stones and looted shops in Milan on Sunday during demonstrations over the rising cost of living, as lorry drivers blocked roads across southern Italy in protest at soaring fuel prices linked to the war in Iran.
Demonstrators light flares during an anti-government protest organised by the Italia in Piazza movement, at Piazza Duomo in Milan. Photograph: Piero Cruciatti/AFP/Getty Images Footage showed police in riot gear firing teargas and using water cannon after several hundred hooded protesters smashed shop windows and set bins alight in the area around Piazza del Duomo in central Milan.
Second day of the demonstration "Italia in piazza" against the high cost of living in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Nicola Marfisi/AGF/Shutterstock View image in fullscreen
Second day of the demonstration “Italia in piazza” against the high cost of living in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Alessandro Bremec/NurPhoto/Shutterstock The clashes echoed scenes of violence in neighbouring France , where hundreds of thousands of people angered by rising living costs and declining public services took to the streets on Saturday in some of the country’s most violent protests in years.
Meanwhile, lorry drivers have caused widespread disruption across southern Italy, blocking major roads and creating huge traffic jams in Sicily, Puglia and Calabria in protest at the mounting financial pressure on the haulage industry.
A handwritten sign reading ''Tutto esaurito'' (All sold out) covers the diesel and petrol price board at a self-service petrol station in Barletta, Italy. Photograph: Matteo Della Torre/NurPhoto/Shutterstock Hundreds of petrol stations have run out of fuel, while supermarkets are beginning to report shortages of several products, particularly fresh food. Pharmacies have also been affected, raising concerns about disruption to essential supplies.
“We are asking the government to urgently convene a meeting with industry associations. Otherwise, we will consider a nationwide strike,” said Pasquale Russo, president of Conftrasporto, an association representing Italian haulage companies.
“The situation is very concerning because the excessive price increases affecting the haulage industry have created a highly tense situation among transport companies, leading to spontaneous protests in some areas of the country,” he added.
In several parts of southern Italy, petrol stations have run out of both petrol and diesel, with motorists facing long queues at the few filling stations still operating. The road blockades have also caused extensive traffic disruption, with fears growing that prolonged protests could further disrupt supplies of food, fuel and other essential goods.
Fuel pumps are cordoned off with red and white barrier tape at a Q8 Easy self-service station in Barletta, Italy. Photograph: Matteo Della Torre/NurPhoto/Shutterstock French president Emmanuel Macron has declined interior minister Laurent Nuñez’s offer to resign after widespread riots over the weekend, despite “failures in anticipation and intelligence” that were deemed to worsen the crisis engulfing the country.
Macron said Nuñez should stay to ‌ensure a chain of command is retained within the police and security forces, and has also asked Nuñez to set up ‌as quickly as possible new approaches to deal with looters, ​the Élysée Palace added, quoted by Reuters.
Macron has asked the minister to “put in place new operational methods for security forces” to get a grip over the crisis.
Saturday, one of the most violent days of unrest in years, left public buildings burned, hundreds of shops destroyed and looted and at least 2,400 people arrested in cities across the country .
In a TV interview on Monday, Nuñez admitted that the police “lost control” in some cities during the protests.
Riots in central Paris over the weekend, with 2,400 people arrested nationally. Photograph: Poitout Florian/ABACA/Shutterstock In an address to the nation last night, Macron criticised “unbridled violence”.
Emmanuel Macron condemns violence in televised address – video
“Watching these images, we have all asked ourselves how could French people do this to France ? ” he said.
Macron criticises ‘unbridled violence’ after days of unrest and 2,400 arrests across France
Separately, I will also be keeping an eye on Ukraine , after another night of heavy attacks by Russia killing at least one and injuring eight people, and with conflicting reports about whether Kyiv authorities had been warned about the strikes.
A police officer inspects the site of a fitness club building hit by a Russian missile strike, amid Russia’s attack on Ukraine, in Kyiv, Ukraine. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters We should also get an update from Sweden , as the parliament’s speaker is set to decide on the next step in the government formation process there.
It’s Monday, 19 October 2026 , it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live .

</details>

Two Greenpeace activists were arrested in Australia after they caused a halt in operations at Woodside Energy's Scarborough LNG platform, off the coast of Western Australia. The two were charged with "interfering with offshore petroleum installations or operations", the local authorities said in a statement as cited by ABC. The maximum penalty for that charge is 10 years in prison.
The two activists boarded the platform on Saturday and spent the night there, Reuters reported , citing a Greenpeace statement that the activists would "occupy the platform for as long as possible" in expression of protest against a new project led by Woodside that, according to the group, would damage a coral reef system that is inhabited by an endangered species of whale. The company disagrees, saying there will be no contact between its production facilities and the coral ecosystem.
The Browse project is estimated to cost around $34 billion to develop, tapping into the largest undeveloped natural gas deposit in Australia, according to ABC.
The Scarborough LNG facility is 98% complete. With a price tag of $12.5 billion , the facility is planned to produce some 8 million tons of liquefied natural gas annually, plus 6 million cum of natural gas, to be supplied to the domestic market. Per schedule, Scarborough was supposed to go into operation before the end of this year, but the two activists' actions this last weekend may prompt a postponement, based on Woodside's comments on the events and the fact that operations at the facility remained on pause.
"Greenpeace's actions have triggered a significant, potentially costly and entirely avoidable response, requiring Western Australian resources to be mobilised to manage the safety, security and operational consequences of their actions. Contrary to Greenpeace's claimed mission, the behaviour of its representatives&hellip; has endangered the surrounding environment, in addition to people and the facility itself," a spokesperson for the energy major told Upstream.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Federal Reserve raises interest rates for the 1st time since 2023
Trump criticized the moving, calling on the Fed to "LOWER THE INTEREST RATES."
The Federal Reserve raised interest rates on Wednesday in an effort to battle back a monthslong surge of inflation set off by the Iran war . The move marks the central bank&#x27;s first rate increase since July 2023.
The central bank hiked its benchmark rate a quarter of a percentage point, putting interest rates at a level between 3.75% and 4%.
Hours after the announcement, President Donald Trump criticized the move, repeating a call for lower interest rates that he has made on multiple occasions since his return to office last year.
The Federal Reserve Building is the headquarters of the Federal Reserve System and 12 Federal Reserve Banks, in Washington, D.C.
"LOWER THE INTEREST RATES FOR THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA, AND FAST!" Trump said in a social media post.
However later, Trump told ABC News that despite the Fed doing the opposite of what he has demanded he still has confidence in Fed Chair Kevin Warsh.
"I’m relying on Kevin, but he’s got a very tough board," Trump said from the tarmac while traveling to a campaign rally in North Carolina, referring to the 12-member policymaking board. "He&#x27;s got a board that was put there by a lot of other people, and the interest rates are too high, they’re not appropriate."
Trump insisted the Fed raised rates “only for political reasons” and to hurt him.
“They’re raising the rates to make Trump do as bad as he can possibly do," he said. "The problem they have is that we have the greatest economy in history."
Earlier Wednesday, the board, including Trump-appointed Warsh, voted unanimously in favor of the rate increase.
That current level of interest rates marks a significant drop from a recent peak attained in 2023, but borrowing costs remain well above a 0% rate established at the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic.
Iran live updates: Sticker shock woes rise after Saudi refinery stops crude loading
The major stock indexes turned lower after the policy announcement. The Dow Jones Industrial Average closed down 630 points, or 1.2%, while the S & P 500 dropped 0.4%. The tech-heavy Nasdaq ticked down 0.01%.
"The plain fact is that inflation is too high and has been for too long," Warsh said at a press conference in Washington, D.C., on Wednesday. "The committee’s unanimous vote shows our resolve to achieve price stability on a timelier basis."
The move appears aimed at containing a recent bout of price increases. Global oil prices are hovering near a four-month high and the average price for a gallon of gasoline tops $4.30, according to AAA .
The U.S. economy has shown signs of additional strain in recent days, including a bond selloff that is pushing up borrowing costs for credit cards and mortgages.
The price woes previously divided central bankers eager to contain inflation but reluctant to cool off the economy and weaken the labor market.
The Fed opted to hold interest rates steady at its most recent meeting in July. Three of the 12 members on the Fed&#x27;s policymaking board, however, voted in favor of a rate hike, marking the largest number of dissenters casting ballots in the same direction in a decade.
Bend, Oregon, shooting: Suspect ID&#x27;d, had attended elementary school, police say
2 dead, 11 injured after Kansas mother drives onto soccer field during game: Police
Atlanta school worker dies after falling from Six Flags roller coaster in Georgia
Warsh, who took the helm of the central bank in May, has vowed to cool off persistently elevated price increases.
"The Fed&#x27;s predominant focus right now should be on prices," Warsh said in remarks last month at the Fed&#x27;s annual summer gathering in Jackson Hole, Wyoming.
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh speaks during a news conference following Federal Open Market Committee meetings at Federal Reserve Headquarters, September 16, 2026 in Washington.
Prices rose 3.4% in August compared to a year earlier, maintaining the same level from the prior month, federal government data last week showed.
Inflation stands more than a percentage point higher than the Federal Reserve&#x27;s target rate of 2%.
Despite a stubborn bout of inflation, the economy remains fairly robust by some measures.
A blockbuster jobs report earlier this month showed employers added 162,000 workers in August, demonstrating continued resilience for the nation&#x27;s labor market. The economy grew over three months ending in June, defying fear of a downturn triggered by the Iran war.
Fighting in the Middle East kept global crude prices above $105 a barrel as of Wednesday, which amounted to a rise of about 50% since the Iran war broke out in late February.
Inflation holds steady at elevated level as Iran war pushes up gas prices
The average price of a gallon of gas in the U.S. stands at $4.36, putting it more than $1.30 higher than before the war, AAA data showed.
Record-high diesel prices have raised transport costs for many everyday products, including groceries, clothes and furniture.
The war in Iran, which began with a large-scale U.S.-Israeli attack in the winter, prompted Iran&#x27;s near-closure of the Strait of Hormuz, which facilitates one-fifth of global crude supply. Saudi Arabia over the weekend shut down a key pipeline bypassing the strait, which further constrained oil delivery and put upward pressure on prices.
ABC News&#x27; Karen Travers, Michelle Stoddart and Devin Garbitt contributed to this report.

### 57. ★★★☆☆ On Iran, Israel’s opposition is even more hawkish than Netanyahu

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T11:35:52+00:00
- **发生了什么**: In rest of the world, the Israeli PM is often blamed for the Iran war. His opponents say he hasn&#039;t been tough enough.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share On Iran, Israel’s opposition is even more hawkish than Netanyahu on social media
From left, Israeli opposition leaders Yair Lapid, Naftali Bennett, Gadi Eisenkot, Avigdor Lieberman and Yair Golan [Getty Images]
When Israel launched a sweeping military assault on Iran with the United States in February, the Israeli government enjoyed overwhelming support from the Jewish Israeli public.
Opposition parties lent their support to the army and backed the audacious step. Enmity towards the government in Iran is an issue mainstream Zionist parties agree on.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Fighting intensifies across Yemen as humanitarian crisis deepens
list 2 of 3 GCC condemns Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabia as Yemen fighting intensifies
list 3 of 3 Hezbollah funding plan indicates a new challenge to Lebanon’s government
Then in June, Israel’s role in the confrontation halted abruptly with an announcement from US President Donald Trump, who said Iran and Israel had agreed to stop attacking each other.
That announcement – and Netanyahu’s decision to abide by it at the time – were unpopular in Israel. According to the Israel Democracy Institute , 65 to 70 percent of Jewish Israelis believe that stopping the war did not serve Israel’s national security interests.
As Israelis prepare to vote in national elections on October 27, Iran remains an important election issue for many. Opposition parties present Iran as an example of what they say are Netanyahu’s many failures. They promise to reset security and foreign policy if they’re elected.
The frontrunner, Gadi Eisenkot, says the conflict is unfinished. While saying that a change in Iran’s government was an unrealistic goal, Eisenkot accuses Netanyahu of doing little to dismantle Iran’s nuclear programme. In September at the inauguration of his new party, Yashar, Eisenkot told supporters: “Ultimately, it must end in the destruction or dismantling of the Iranian nuclear programme, a goal not yet achieved with 440kg [970lb] of highly enriched uranium already there, which could quickly be turned into several nuclear devices.”
Former Prime Minister Yair Lapid, who is now the head of the opposition in the Knesset, accuses Netanyahu of a strategic failure. He recently told the Atlantic Council that toppling the administration in Tehran is a realistic objective that requires a military escalation: “What Israel should do or the alliance should do is bomb all the energy facilities, especially on Kharg Island, but other places as well in order to make sure that the Iranian economy is demolished. And the reason is that it’s the only thing that will overturn the regime.”
Bombing this civilian infrastructure was actually something Netanyahu had lobbied for himself. An international outcry about such bombings likely constituting war crimes and Trump’s refusal to offer backing put the brakes on the plan.
Naftali Bennett, another former prime minister, says Israel must ensure its military dominance over the entire region. For Bennett, toppling the government in Iran and crushing it is a must. But he says that is not enough. The right-wing politician calls Qatar an “enemy” and says Turkiye is “the new Iran”, going as far as saying that military confrontation with the NATO member was inevitable.
“We will build an alliance of moderate forces in the region against the Islamist forces, against Iran, against Qatar, Turkiye and all the radical forces. We will take proactive actions to shape the region,” Bennett recently told supporters.
Eisenkot and other opposition leaders do not rule out renewed confrontations. They focus on their confidence in the military and security establishment in assessing the dangers and dealing with them appropriately.
But opposition leaders have voiced concern that Netanyahu might resort to military escalation before the elections to tilt the balance in his favour or even disrupt them altogether – even though the prime minister said last week that he did not plan to attack Iran before the vote.
Those fears were elevated after Netanyahu posted a video from the Tel Nof Airbase warning that Israel’s enemies might attack before the elections. Other news reports said Netanyahu has shared intelligence on new threats from Iran with the US president.
Netanyahu was quickly rebuked by opposition leaders, who accused him of “political fearmongering”.
Lapid openly questioned Netanyahu’s motives. Lapid also accused him of using the Israeli military and bases as “backdrops” for election campaign videos.
Ultimately, most Israeli opposition parties and leaders aren’t opposed to war with Iran, and many are in fact even more hawkish in their approach towards Tehran than Netanyahu. They just don’t want a new military escalation before the elections.

</details>

### 59. ★★★☆☆ Iran support for Houthis probed as Yemen war re-erupts and Saudi cities hit - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T10:57:02+00:00
- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMiuAFBVV95cUxOVlNET3hzZkR6WThWY0ZMbkpUYjVCY1pqWG5GQm1CSlNiXzNzWXJTbFAyZEhROXljekhHenNVcEZfVjgyQ3VvcmRuS2VNWjVvYVRMWGxaaXVoUEZPZFZNV1NPUHBtRUFRMUZCTWhTT2g3cEtDZWJabGVLdDhDVGN0UjJwNGVCUWZGVnFYZ2Z3WkZqVllVWDI2eHRZaXozY1dLdVdyUjd5Q2ZuNlJOaTFhUF9pVEF5VnR00gG-AUFVX3lxTFBzaVFoRXFEcWhGVFVLaGo5OGUwWnNicXhwSzdhQ0lJT3VfNk5ySFBXcFhpQkotdnpKNzBSeWVJd3ZKaTBFRE9aRmFfTjcxRk5fLXZfdU5oX205VWVNX1dzS0g1R0RjTG9nTXptLWNKWUcwQW1VN1VwOF9QTkNGaUE2SGFYcFB2N1lDcmdzbzJUS0pMZ0

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Iran support for Houthis probed as Yemen war re-erupts and Saudi cities hit on social media
Houthi supporters rally in Sanaa, Yemen, while carrying Iranian flags and posters featuring former Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei [File: Khaled Abdullah/Reuters]
New allegations about Iranian support for Yemen’s Houthis have emerged in media reports and official comments, highlighting the opaque nature of the alliance.
Media outlets have reported that Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) aided the Houthis as they seized swaths of western Yemen and launched their most potent and sophisticated attacks yet on Saudi Arabia, which supports the internationally recognised Yemeni government fighting the Houthis.
Iranian support for the Houthis reported by the media includes training, intelligence, tactical guidance and help in targeting for drone and missile attacks.
If true, this will undoubtedly escalate tensions between Iran and Saudi Arabia, given that the kingdom has been targeted by dozens of Houthi drones and missiles in recent weeks, including an attack on King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh that killed 13 people. Saudi energy facilities and oil tankers have also been targeted by the group.
Iranian backing, according to the reports, contributed to a September offensive by the Houthis, an armed group also known as Ansar Allah (Arabic for Supporters of God). Those battles saw the Houthis win control of Yemen’s Red Sea coastline and the strategically important Bab al-Mandeb strait.
The Wall Street Journal reported that Iranian Mahan Air flights ferried IRGC paramilitary officers, drone parts, missile guidance systems and impact detonators to the Houthis in Yemen. IRGC officers were also reportedly on the front lines directing Houthi attacks.
The spokesperson for the Saudi-led coalition supporting Yemen’s government, Major-General Turki al-Maliki, confirmed the presence of an external state actor behind the Houthis’ evolving arsenal but did not mention the state in question.
Al-Maliki noted that the Houthis, who have been at war with the government since 2014, have acquired advanced technologies with regional assistance.
This support has made the Houthis the first nonstate armed group to deploy ballistic missiles, offensive and suicide drones, and explosive-laden, high-speed boats, he said, although other Iranian allies, such as Hezbollah and Iranian militias, also possess such weaponry.
While the Houthis have stressed their independence from Iran, the two groups are ideologically aligned. They have also been accused of collaborating closely on military matters, evident in the Houthis’ missile arsenal.
Yemenis protest in support of the Houthis in Sanaa while carrying placards that read, “God is great. Death to America. Death to Israel. Curse the Jews. Victory to Islam” [File: Osamah Abdulrahman/AP] Operational planning
Analysts who spoke to Al Jazeera all agreed that the “regional assistance” the Houthis are receiving points towards the IRGC although Iran and the Houthis denied this.
One indication of this is the bombing of Sanaa International Airport in July before an Iranian plane carrying a Houthi delegation travelling from Tehran was due to land. The Yemeni government, which said its forces carried out the attack, accused “the Iranian aircraft” of violating Yemeni airspace.
“[Recent reports] confirm what is known to experts for years as the Revolutionary Guard and Hezbollah experts engaged in managing the operational planning of battles against the Yemeni government and neighbouring countries, including the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia,” Ibrahim Jalal, a Yemen-focused analyst, told Al Jazeera.
“For example, the killing of Hassan Irloo, the Revolutionary Guard envoy to the Houthis, in Yemen after the military campaign on Marib in 2021 is one of the important indicators, in addition to Mahan aviation flights that transported more experts ahead of the Houthis’ internal and external escalation during July of this year.”
Ali al-Dahab, a military and strategic security affairs expert, agreed that the advanced weaponry used by the Houthis indicates an Iranian role in recent operations.
“What was leaked about Iranian support for the Houthis through this report did not add anything new except that it confirmed the confirmed, and what it added perhaps is that weapons smuggling, especially precision technologies for missiles and unmanned aircraft, takes place via small submarines,” al-Dahab told Al Jazeera.
“This issue is not everything being smuggled from Iran to the Houthis because there are interceptions of smuggled shipments revealed by the joint forces that were controlling the Mocha coast to Bab al-Mandeb before the Houthis’ control and before its break in September.”
Al-Dahab pointed out that while smugglers historically have used dhows, traditional wooden sailboats, to mislead naval coalitions, employing submarines is a new method used by the Houthis, akin to the tactics used by organised criminal groups in the Americas.
“It indicates that Iran focuses on the Houthis as the centre of military and security gravity south of the Red Sea and, therefore, provides them with these weapons, ammunition, expertise and information in anticipation of a battle that may break out in the Strait of Hormuz,” al-Dahab said. “Therefore, the Houthis play a supporting role for them in the Bab al-Mandeb strait.”
He warned of broader implications for international shipping with the Houthis now in control of Bab al-Mandeb, a narrow waterway linking the Gulf of Aden and the Red Sea.
“The greatest danger now is that substate entities in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden region unite under Iran’s leadership to confront the United States, Britain and Europe in general if a battle breaks out in the Gulf of Aden and the strait,” al-Dahab said.
Saudi Arabia and Iran had appeared to have patched up years of hostilities, including the suspension of diplomatic relations, with a China-brokered reconciliation agreement signed in Beijing in 2023.
Hesham Alghannam, a Riyadh-based fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, said he doubts whether Saudi Arabia ever read the Beijing agreement as reconciliation but as a way of lowering tensions. Differences on geopolitical issues have continued between the two countries since the agreement was signed, including on Yemen, which lies on Saudi Arabia’s southern border.
“That isn’t a breach you can take to a lawyer. It’s keeping two files apart, and it works until someone says it out loud. That’s what’s changed,” Alghannam told Al Jazeera.
“Honestly, the deeper question was never whether Tehran signs [the agreement with Saudi Arabia]. It’s whether anything Tehran signs reaches the people holding the launchers,” he added, referring to Iran-aligned armed groups, such as the Houthis.
“We know how the other sequence ends. In 2014, the [Houthis] were offered seats, special status for Saada [the northern Yemeni stronghold of the Houthis] and a share of the executive authority in Yemen in return for handing the heavy weapons to the state,” he added.
What followed was the Houthis seizure of the capital, Sanaa, in September 2014, forcing the government to flee south, and the later killing of former President Ali Abdullah Saleh.
“What followed was the seizure of the institutions, the president detained, parliament dissolved. The gains were banked, and the guns were kept,” Alghannam added.
“Saleh partnered with them, and he was killed in the street in 2017. Even after taking Sanaa, they didn’t become the state. They built a network of supervisors sitting over every official post. So the Saudi red line is one thing, no armed group or second army inside one state.”
The Saudi-led intervention in Yemen began in March 2015 and led to another direct conflict between Riyadh and the Houthis. Despite the massive support Saudi Arabia provided the internationally recognised government, it failed to retake Sanaa. This highlighted weaknesses within the anti-Houthi coalition, including divisions between the internationally recognised government and southern separatists.
“The lesson of 2015 wasn’t that acting firmly leads to a long war. It was that announcing a goal earlier than you should [do] does because once it’s said out loud, you’re bound to it long before you can deliver it,” he said.
“What Riyadh is trying to do now is easy to say and hard to do, but it’s crucial: tie the money and the weapons to real integration under one command, not to declared loyalty.”
A Yemeni family uses a shop in Taiz province in southwestern Yemen as a temporary shelter on October 8, 2026, after being displaced by renewed fighting between government forces and the Houthis [AFP] Strategic independence
Hassan Ahmadian, an assistant professor of political science at the University of Tehran, told Al Jazeera that Iran seeks technological independence for partners rather than perpetual reliance.
“This pattern is not like the United States pattern, which keeps whoever follows and allies with it in need of it, meaning [they have] to buy from the United States the weapons he needs, and the main weapon is basically not given to him if it is an Arab or Islamic country specifically,” he said.
“As for Iran, the ally is provided with the technology to create and produce the weapon, and this is not only in Yemen but in other regions and even in Gaza as much as possible where the technology was transferred because the view is different.”
Ahmadian said enabling Iran’s allies to produce their own weapons is a strategic move by Tehran and the Houthis are also motivated by breaking the Saudi siege on territories it controls in Yemen.
“The view is that this technology helps in confronting a common enemy, and therefore, strengthening this capability independently of Iran is in itself a strategic interest for Iran,” Ahmadian said.
“Notice that there is an advance for Ansar Allah [the Houthis] linked to the justice of the cause as there is a siege imposed on the Yemenis affecting 70 percent to 80 percent of the society controlled by Ansar Allah and the Sanaa government. So this cause, breaking the siege, is a just cause. … The regional circumstance gave [the Houthis] a motive that this is the time to pressure Saudi Arabia to break the siege, and this is what actually happened.”
